Shown against a real server by `seed-initialization.db.test.ts`: the first run fills an empty database; a repeat, and a database with only one table filled, refuse and change nothing; a failure part-way (a trigger on the last table) leaves nothing and the next run succeeds; two runs at once cannot both fill the tables (the second waits for the first, then refuses; with a lock timeout it is ended by it; if the first is rolled back the second fills the database once); and **the provisioning login, with the seed label asserted, is refused an `INSERT` of a grant into an existing bundle by the privilege**, which was reproduced as succeeding on the migrations up to 0004. The runtime and the base worker are refused both functions.
- *The reference seeds against their documents (`tools/seed/`).* 127 permission keys, equal to the keys parsed from `permissions.md` section 7 and to the total that document states; 1 hub; 6 bundles (Ops Staff, Senior Ops, Platform Admin, Finance/Reconciliation, Rider, Vendor) with 36 grants, each with an explicit hub scope, ownership scope and tier, and revision 1 / floor 1; the two fixed policy rows; 31 settings (the `CONFIRMED` keys of `settings.md` sections 7.1, 7.5 and 7.7, each value compared with the cell of the document, money in pesewas, per-tier values as named objects; no `OPEN` key); the twelve identity reasons equal to the table of `domain-model.md` section 3.9 (code, domain, label, operations; four branch-qualified), every operation checked against the operations of `openapi.yaml` (OB-74). The database test reads the rows back and compares them with the same documents, and the API runtime reads them as a staff context and as the `authentication` capability.
- *The command and the fixtures.* `pnpm --filter @melarc/api run db:seed` (`DATABASE_SEED_URL` for the provisioning login, with `APP_ENV`; without them the local development database with the local provisioning login, refused when `APP_ENV` names a deployed environment). The URL's user must be the provisioning login; the URL and its password are never printed. `DATABASE_SEED_URL` is **forbidden in the API's environment**, like `DATABASE_MIGRATION_URL`. `--fixtures` writes two test-only reasons through `melarc.add_reason_fixtures` (`tools/fixtures/`: `FIXTURE_REASON_INACTIVE`, `FIXTURE_REASON_OTHER_OPERATION`, labelled `Fixture:`) and **refuses production, and any value that is not a known non-production environment, before it connects** (the database does not know its environment, so that refusal stays the command's; the function itself writes nothing but `FIXTURE_` codes). The built tool was run as a process against a disposable database: it seeded (exit 0), refused a second run (exit 1, nothing on stdout), refused `--fixtures` with `APP_ENV=production` (exit 1) and wrote the fixtures with `APP_ENV=staging`, each ending by itself, with no password in any output. `melarc_dev` was not touched.
- *Targeted breaks.* Twenty-eight single breaks were run (a shared key kept shared, no emptiness check, no locks, a short insert kept, a table named twice, another capability opened, a table written as soon as it is counted, a grant before its bundle, the fixed-policy keys not taken, no `INSERT` for the provisioning login, `UPDATE` granted to it, an insert policy without its capability test or naming another, a seed read policy missing or with an `ELSE true`, `INSERT` granted to the API runtime, the tier left `NOT NULL`, production allowed for the fixtures, an unescaped fixture pattern, the command without its fixtures guard, its login check, its `APP_ENV` requirement or its local-default refusal, the API accepting `DATABASE_SEED_URL`, the matrix without the seed's `SELECT`, the seed wiring off the composition-root list). Twenty-seven were caught at once. The twenty-eighth, the floor condition of the fixed-policy insert policy, could not be: with revision 1 the table's own range check already makes the floor 1, so it was an equivalent mutant; the redundant floor conditions were removed from both policies, and the revision condition of each was then broken and caught (two more). Every file was restored and its hash checked.
- *Gate (B D E, uncached, Windows, once on the final state of S0.9; superseded by the gate on the final tree of the correction batch, [§7.12](#712-stage-1-correction-batch-10-october-2026-disposition)).* `format:check`, `lint`, `typecheck`, `build`, `contract:check`, `api-client:check` and `db:check` exit 0; `pnpm test` exits 0 (root 1189 with 2 skipped, API 3576 with 5 skipped, Ops 220, api-client 250, harness 199 with 1 skipped); `pnpm run test:db` exits 0 (583 passed and 1 skipped in 18 files, the new `seed.db.test.ts` being 28 tests as the provisioning and runtime logins); `pnpm run test:e2e` exits 0 (16 passed). **Not run:** the Linux, WSL2 and CI runs (OB-62), `db:seed` against `melarc_dev` (the owner runs `db:migrate` and then `db:seed`; OB-82) and `pnpm audit` (once, at the end of the stage).
**Files (as built).** `migrations/0004_seed_policies.sql` with its snapshot and journal entry, `platform/database/schema/reference-data.ts` (tier nullable), `platform/database/seed/seed-helper.ts`, `tools/seed/` (`permission-keys.ts`, `bundles.ts`, `hubs.ts`, `reasons.ts`, `settings.ts`, `reference-seed.ts`, `seed-command.ts`, `seed-runtime.ts`), `tools/fixtures/`, `tools/db-seed.ts` and the `db:seed` script, `platform/config/load-config.ts` (`DATABASE_SEED_URL` forbidden), `platform/database/capability-access.ts` (the seed's `SELECT`), `src/test-support/seed-documents.ts`, `architecture.test.ts` (the composition-root list now names `tools/seed/seed-runtime.ts`), the unit tests beside each file, `test/database/seed.db.test.ts` (and `reference-data.db.test.ts`, whose provisioning pin changed deliberately), `test/support/api-process.ts`, DEVELOPMENT.md, DEPLOYMENT_AND_ENVIRONMENTS.md, MIGRATION_AND_SEEDING.md and [apps/api/CLAUDE.md](../../apps/api/CLAUDE.md).
**Obligations carried.** `GBT-E6` and `GBT-E7`: **ran at S0.9 on the real seed tables and the real provisioning role**; repeated for the final surface in S0.64: **D, not run**. §13.10a obligation 5 (the seed follows the one lock protocol) ran at S0.9 against the real policy keys; the rest are in [§7.10](#710-carried-from-s09).
**Does not.** Seed an identity (S0.30) or invent a setting value the documents leave unset.
**Stop if.** A setting has no approved value.

#### S0.10 — Integration kit (Gate 1)

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.9, S0.6 |
| **Run**| B D |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Builds on.** `src/test-support/` (test application, test configuration, captured logs, contract fixtures), the `database` Vitest project and its `test-database.ts`, which migrates a fresh disposable `melarc_test_<hex>` database for each file and removes only what it made, and the test-only `rls-fixture.ts` table.
**Newly builds.** A real application and real PostgreSQL per file, extending that mechanism (a template database only if the measured migration cost justifies it); an injectable `Clock`; a `TestClient` (cookie jar, CSRF echo, bearer); `admin()` SQL (age rows, break a write, change a status); `observe()`, a second-connection reader; the capture reader; a work-shape tracer; a pool of one.
**Files.** `src/test-support/*`, `apps/api/test/database/support/*`, the Vitest configuration, and the kit's own tests.
**Evidence.** The kit's own tests: aging a row by SQL, a refusal read from a second connection, the capture reader empty by default. **Gate 1:** `pnpm run test:db` green on the developer's database. **Reached (10 October 2026), as `melarc_api_runtime` for the application and the cluster administrator for `admin()` and `observe()`:**
- *The application, real, over a real database of its own.* `startIntegrationKit(database)` (`test/database/support/integration.ts`) loads the runtime configuration (`loadConfig` over `validEnv`), starts the real application through `startTestApp` and `AppModule` on an ephemeral port, connected as `melarc_api_runtime` over a **pool of one** (the pool's `max` is 1, and the second connection sees one backend of that login), and gives a `TestClient` for it. `/readyz` answers 200 through the client; a path nothing serves answers the contract's envelope with `Cache-Control: no-store`; two requests in a row on the pool of one both succeed. Several kits run at once, each over a database of its own.
- *An injectable `Clock`, and no production time-travel switch.* The kit's `ManualClock` is bound through the `ports` option of `AppModule.register`, in the kit's composition only; the application resolves that instance, and the client's cookie jar reads it. Three tests pin the other half: no production source file imports `src/test-support/` (an import, a side-effect import, a dynamic import and a `require` are all found), no configuration key names a clock, a fake time, an offset or a time-travel, and `main.ts` passes the module no `ports`.
- *`TestClient` (`src/test-support/test-client.ts`).* A cookie jar that keeps name, path, `Max-Age` and `Expires` (judged by the injected clock), withholds a `Secure` cookie from plain http except to loopback, keeps and sends an `HttpOnly` cookie without being able to echo it; the CSRF echo (`melarc_csrf` in `X-CSRF-Token`, and `Origin`, on every unsafe request, and none on a safe one) with a wrong token, no token, no origin and another origin on demand; a bearer mode that sends `Authorization` and none of a browser's cookie, token or origin and keeps no cookie; no redirect followed.
- *`admin()` and `observe()`.* **Aging a row by SQL:** a hub made now is not two days old by the database's clock; after `ageRows('hub', 'created_at', '2 days', { id })` the second connection reads that it is, and another row is untouched. **A refusal read from a second connection:** the application's login is refused `INSERT` into `hub` (`42501`) and the second connection counts the same rows and none named for the refused insert. `breakWrites('reason_code')` makes the real seed fail with `P0001` in its last step, every table written before it is rolled back (read from the second connection), and after `mend` the seed completes. `observe()` is read-only at the server (`25006`) and does not wait for the application's connection. Both refuse a table or column name that is not a plain identifier, and take every value as a parameter.
- *The capture reader.* Empty by default; read-only (the reader has no `push` or `clear`); a copy on every read. Nothing writes to its `captureSink` until the capture adapter of S0.17 is bound.
- *The work-shape tracer (`src/test-support/work-shape.ts`).* Records the statements the application's login sent while some work ran, each with its values taken out, and none of the administrator's or the observer's; the driver is put back on return and on a throw; one trace at a time. Against the real service: a transaction reads as `begin … commit` with the statement between, two runs that differ only in a value have one shape, and a run that issues one more statement has another.
- *Stop if: parallel files cannot each have a disposable database.* They can: two `createTestDatabase()` calls made at the same moment give two migrated databases, an application over each, and neither sees the other's rows. The `database` project still runs its files one at a time, for the reason stated in `vitest.config.ts` (the roles are cluster-wide and some tests change them and put them back); that is a choice, not a limit. **A template database is not justified:** creating and migrating a database costs a median of 205 ms and 215 ms (six runs on this machine, Windows, PostgreSQL 18.6 in the local container) and dropping it 85 ms, so a template would save about 0.2 s a file of a run of about 150 s. The Vitest configuration is unchanged.
- *Targeted breaks.* Twenty-three single breaks (no CSRF echo, `Secure` always withheld, expiry read from the machine's clock, an `HttpOnly` token echoed, a path matched by prefix alone, redirects followed, the tracer recording every login, the driver left patched, two traces at once, values left in the shape, the capture store handed out or not cleared, a writable observer, an unchecked identifier, aging that reaches every row, a broken write that does not fail or is never mended, the clock not injected, a larger pool, a production file importing test support, a time setting among the configuration keys, a port binding in `main.ts`). Twenty-two were caught at once; the twenty-third, a bearer client keeping the cookies it is sent, was not, because its test looked only at what the client *sent*; the test now looks at the jar, and the break is caught. Every file was restored and its hash checked.
- *The adversarial review of Stage 1.* Three independent reviewers read S0.6, S0.7 to S0.8 with the policy part of S0.9, and S0.9 with S0.10, without editing anything. They were stopped after a few minutes by the instruction to wrap up, found no critical or high defect in the code they read, and proved nothing by running it; every lead below was checked against the code and the database before anything was changed, and each one fixed has a test that failed first. **Fixed:** (1) a statement sent by a continuation the work left behind, while a failed transaction was being rolled back or while the commit was in flight, queued behind it and ran on the connection with no transaction and no context (`database.service.ts` now closes the handle when the work settles); (2) a cancel connection that never finished closing kept its place for ever (its close has the cancel's bound); (3) the guard on `role_bundle` let the `privileged` flag change without a raised revision and the `id` change at all, and `permitted_operations` accepted a NULL element (all three in `0004_seed_policies.sql`, with the drizzle schema and snapshot in step); (4) the grants check did not look for `TRUNCATE`, `REFERENCES`, `TRIGGER` or `MAINTAIN` (PostgreSQL 17 on), which no login may hold on any table; (5) the `db:seed` local default did not refuse a server that is not on this machine, and a pool that failed to close could hide why the seed refused; (6) the seed-data test could not tell a Senior Ops grant from a Platform Admin one on the rows that state two reaches; (7) `DATABASE_ACQUIRE_TIMEOUT_MS` is documented as two bounds, as it is. **Recorded for the owner and not closed at that time:** OB-83 and OB-84 (the workflow reasons; the emptiness of a seed target is not the database's rule), OB-85 to OB-87 below. OB-83 to OB-85 are closed or decided by the correction batch ([§7.12](#712-stage-1-correction-batch-10-october-2026-disposition)). **Verified sound by reading, per the reviewers:** the execution budget (timers, listeners, children), the connection queue, the lease and the abandoned connect, the commit-in-flight path, the lock planner, acquisition and the statement counting, the policy NULL handling, the 127 permission keys, the 36 grants, the 31 settings and the 12 reasons against their documents. Nine more single breaks were run on the fixes (the handle left open, an unbounded close, the guard without its two new conditions, no NULL check, the grants check without the four privileges, the local default without its host check, a close that hides the reason, a Platform Admin scope narrowed) and each was caught.
- *Corrected on 10 October 2026 (F06; [§7.12](#712-stage-1-correction-batch-10-october-2026-disposition)).* A start that fails part-way closes what it had opened (the application, the administrator's connection, the observer's), last opened first, and reports the failure itself or, if a close failed too, both; `stop()` attempts every close whatever an earlier one did, reports every failure (one as itself, several as an `AggregateError`) and does nothing a second time; `Admin.close()` ends its connection even when a table cannot be mended; `startTestApp` closes the application it built when it is refused. The database the kit was given is never closed or dropped by it.
- *Gate 1 (B D E, uncached, Windows, once on the final state of S0.10, after the adversarial review of the stage; superseded by the gate on the final tree of the correction batch, [§7.12](#712-stage-1-correction-batch-10-october-2026-disposition)).* `format:check`, `lint`, `typecheck`, `build`, `contract:check`, `api-client:check` and `db:check` exit 0; `pnpm test` exits 0 (root 1189 with 2 skipped, API 3631 with 5 skipped, Ops 220, api-client 250, harness 199 with 1 skipped); `pnpm run test:db` exits 0 (606 passed and 1 skipped in 19 files, the new `integration-kit.db.test.ts` being 16 tests as the runtime login and the administrator; Gate 1's condition held on that tree: a real application and a real PostgreSQL are driven from a test as `melarc_api_runtime`, on the Windows machine's local PostgreSQL 18.6 container, which is the developer's database service); `pnpm run test:e2e` exits 0 (16 passed). `pnpm audit --audit-level high` exits 0 (one moderate advisory, the esbuild one of the open owner items). **Not run:** the Linux, WSL2 and CI runs (OB-62), `test:browser`, `db:seed` against `melarc_dev` (OB-82).
**Files (as built).** `src/test-support/test-client.ts`, `work-shape.ts` and `capture.ts` with their tests, `src/test-support/integration-kit.test.ts`, `test/database/support/admin.ts`, `observe.ts` and `integration.ts`, `test/database/integration-kit.db.test.ts`, [apps/api/CLAUDE.md](../../apps/api/CLAUDE.md) (the kit, in its Tests paragraph).
**Obligations carried.** None of the §13.10a obligations belongs to this task. The kit's own are in [§7.11](#711-carried-from-s010).
**Does not.** Add a production time-travel switch.
**Stop if.** Parallel test files cannot each have a disposable database of their own.

### Stage 2 — Cross-cutting services

Everything a credential-issuing operation needs exists, tested, before the first operation: audit, idempotency, settings and reasons, the authoritative limiter, hash admission, metrics and the **durable delivery path**. Ends at **Gate 2** (S0.19).

#### S0.11 — Audit writer

**Gate 2 correction (11 October 2026):** the writer holds every event to the catalogue's tier and, for the identity events whose facts the catalogue states, to those facts (G2-06); `audit.md` is corrected (190 codes, the domains, the grammar, the before and after rule, `recorded_at`). See [§7.15](#715-gate-2-audit-corrections-11-october-2026-disposition).

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.10 |
| **Run**| B D |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Builds on.** `platform/logging/redaction.ts` (redaction of log lines); nothing writes an audit row today.
**Newly builds.** `audit_event` and the `AuditWriter` adapter: append-only (`GBT-T1`), enhanced writes fail closed (`GBT-T10`), stable event ids and a canonical serialisation version, the object-type catalogue and the hub-boundary event, and the **committed-failure shape** authentication bookkeeping needs: a failed sign-in commits its counter and its `auth.session.failed` event, then answers the error outside the transaction ([audit.md](../../contracts/audit.md) §2.1, §5.4).
**Files.** a new migration, `platform/audit/*`, registry row, `contracts/audit.md` only if the catalogue differs.
**Evidence.** DB tests: an `UPDATE` or `DELETE` of an audit row is refused for every login; an enhanced write that cannot be stored aborts the act; redaction removes a planted secret (Family U sentinel). *Fixture/platform verified*.
**Obligations carried.** Signed checkpoints, independent storage and monitoring are release gate **G1** and are not built here.
**Reached (10 October 2026), as `melarc_api_runtime`, the other technical logins and the cluster administrator** (`audit-event.db.test.ts`, 34 tests; `audit-record.test.ts`, 26 tests; `canonical-audit-event.test.ts`, `commit-then-refuse.test.ts`, `audit-event-codes.test.ts`; `transaction-handle.test.ts`, 8 tests, and `transaction-handle.db.test.ts`, 2 tests, over the real service):
- *The ledger.* `audit_event` (`0006_audit_event.sql`; class AUDIT, already classified by the registry row `AuditEvent`) holds the fields of audit.md §3 and three additions the contract implies and does not name (`detail`, `reason_label`, `serialisation_version`; OB-97). Its closed sets (actor, surface, object type, outcome) are repeated as CHECKs, and a test inserts every value the writer knows and refuses one it does not, so the two cannot drift. The system is the null actor and the null actor is the system. Forced row-level security: a staff context inserts an event whose actor is itself and reads the events of its hubs (hub scope `ALL`, or the event's hub among its authorized hubs); a `SYSTEM` context inserts under `authentication` alone and reads nothing; a vendor, a rider and every other capability neither read nor write. The API runtime holds `SELECT` and `INSERT`; the other logins hold nothing; `GRANT_CEILINGS` (`registry-grants.ts`) holds the registry's write cell for `AuditEvent` at `INSERT`.
- *Append-only (`GBT-T1`).* No login holds `UPDATE`, `DELETE` or `TRUNCATE`: the API runtime, the base worker and the provisioning login each get `42501` for all three. The layering is stated as it is. The table owner and the migration identity hold the privileges but are subject to forced row-level security and no policy admits `UPDATE` or `DELETE`, so those change **zero rows**; `TRUNCATE` (which row-level security does not govern) and every row change by the administrator, who bypasses row-level security, are refused by the trigger (`audit_event_no_change`, `audit_event_no_truncate`, message "append-only", `42501`). The plan's earlier phrase "refused for every login" therefore reads: an error for every runtime login and for the administrator, no row changed for the two roles that hold the privilege and are held by the policy.
- *The writer.* `PostgresAuditWriter.write` runs in the caller's transaction through the `TransactionHandle` bridge (`transaction-handle.ts`). **The actor, the surface and the correlation id are the transaction's context's, and that context is the one the database service applied** (`bindTransactionHandle(tx)` takes it from the service's registry of the transaction's facts, never from the code that binds, and the service takes its frozen copy in the step that validates the context): the actor is `auditActorOf(context)`, so a `SYSTEM` transaction is the null actor whatever technical identifier its context carries, and it cannot name that identifier as a person either (OB-47 is closed); a human principal's transaction can record that principal and nobody else; only a `SYSTEM` transaction (sign-in, bootstrap) may name the person it authenticated, and a `SYSTEM` actor never has an id (writer, CHECK and the staff `INSERT` policy). A code outside the catalogue of audit.md §5 (`audit-event-codes.ts`, 190 codes, read against the document by a test) is `AUDIT_EVENT_CODE_UNKNOWN`; an object type outside the closed set, an identifier that is not a uuid, a reason without its label snapshot and a state outside the state machines' form are `AUDIT_EVENT_REFUSED`. **Screening (Family U):** a fact whose name says it is a secret (password, token, TOTP, cookie, CSRF, signature, a whole-word `pin`, `otp` or `seed`) is refused, so is a value that is an email address or an E.164 number, and a secret that travels inside a text (a `Bearer` header, `key=value`, a URL's user information) is removed with the scrubber of the logs; **the correlation id is screened like any text** (it may come from a client's key): an address or a number in it is refused; keys are plain snake_case, objects are bounded in keys, depth and bytes, and a refusal names the field and never the value. A sentinel planted in a note, a detail value and a related-ids value is absent from the stored row (`audit_event::text`).
- *Fail closed (`GBT-T10`).* The insert is a statement of the caller's transaction, so a write that cannot be stored (row-level security, a constraint, a trigger the store breaks) fails the transaction and the act it records does not commit. A caller that catches the failure and carries on is answered `TransactionRolledBackError`, never success (correction F01). **An event the writer itself refuses** (an unknown code, a secret-named field, an address) fails before any statement is sent, which left the transaction healthy; the writer therefore aborts the transaction with a statement the server rejects, and a caller that catches the refusal is answered `TransactionRolledBackError` and the act is not kept (found by the review of this batch, finding 1; three cases in `audit-event.db.test.ts`). It holds for an enhanced event and for any other. The count of an enhanced write that failed closed is `audit_enhanced_write_failed_total` (S0.15); the alert is S0.68's.
- *The committed-failure shape.* `commitThenRefuse(unitOfWork, context, work)`: the work does the bookkeeping (a counter, `auth.session.failed`) in a transaction and **returns** the error; the transaction commits; the error is raised after the commit and outside it. If the bookkeeping cannot be written, that failure is raised and not the refusal. Shown against the real database: the counter and the event of a refused attempt are kept, raising the same refusal inside the transaction keeps neither, and a broken store answers its own failure.
- *What the chain will need (audit.md §2.1).* Stable identifiers (UUIDv7 from the injected clock), `serialisation_version` on every row, and a byte-stable canonical form (`canonical-audit-event.ts`: sorted keys at every depth, UTC microsecond timestamps read with `to_char`, JSON escaping by the language specification; a known answer pins the exact bytes). **The signed checkpoint chain, its independent storage and its monitoring are release gate G1 and are not here; `GBT-T3` to `T9` are open, neither passed nor dropped.**
- *Targeted breaks.* Twelve single breaks in the build, and for the review's fixes the test of each was seen to fail before its change (the refusal that left the transaction healthy, the technical identifier named as a person, the unscreened correlation id, the handle's context taken from the binder). The twelve (the actor check, the catalogue check, the secret-name screen, the text scrub, the refusal thrown inside the transaction, the update grant, the truncate trigger, the staff actor binding, the `SYSTEM` capability test, the staff hub read, a swallowed write failure, and the enhanced-only count) were each caught and each file was restored and its hash checked.
**Does not.** Write any event of a later operation.
**Stop if.** The canonical serialisation cannot be made byte-stable across Node versions.

#### S0.12 — Idempotency and replay store

**Gate 2 correction (11 October 2026):** how a command in progress under its key is observed, and what is still the owner's to decide, is recorded in SECURITY_DESIGN.md §14.16 (§11.D, OB-100); the replay cleanup is served by a partial index (G2-07, migration `0013`). See [§7.15](#715-gate-2-audit-corrections-11-october-2026-disposition).

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.10, S0.3 |
| **Run**| B D |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Newly builds.** `idempotency_record` and the `IdempotencyStore`: principal- and operation-bound keys, fingerprint conflict (`IDEMPOTENCY_KEY_CONFLICT`), an in-progress marker, byte-stable business replay; the **one bounded exception** of [SECURITY_DESIGN.md](../../architecture/SECURITY_DESIGN.md) §14.16 for `registerRiderDevice` (an encrypted secret replayed under a time-limited exception with a 24-hour tombstone and expiry on read); version and ETag helpers. Family I.
**Files.** `platform/idempotency/*`, a new migration, the registry row, and tests.
**Evidence.** DB tests: same key and body replays the stored response; same key and a changed body conflicts; another principal's key never collides; a replayed secret expires on read. *Fixture/platform verified*.
**Reached (10 October 2026), as `melarc_api_runtime`, with the sealed fields observed from the administrator's connection** (`idempotency-record.db.test.ts`, 27 tests; `idempotency-scope.test.ts`, `request-fingerprint.test.ts`, `version-etag.test.ts`):
- *The record.* `idempotency_record` (`0007_idempotency_record.sql`; the registry row `IdempotencyRecord`) is unique on **principal type, principal id, operation id and key**, with the request's fingerprint compared inside that identity (SECURITY_DESIGN.md §14.16). Row-level security: `STAFF`, `VENDOR` and `RIDER` read, insert and update the records whose principal is themselves and no others (the type is part of the identity, so a vendor with a staff member's id sees nothing of the staff member's); `SYSTEM` under `authentication` reaches a record **only to erase its sealed replay** and inserts none (the context in which a consuming transaction erases a grant's sealed replay; the registry: "erasing the sealed columns only"): it reads the records that hold or held a sealed replay, updates only those that still hold one, and the update must leave none and set the tombstone, so it reads no other saved answer, completes no command in progress and cannot lengthen the window of a replay (review finding 3; the policy of `SELECT` also admits the tombstone because PostgreSQL judges the row an `UPDATE` leaves behind against it, and without that the erasure itself is refused); every other context reads and writes nothing. Grants: `SELECT`, `INSERT` and `UPDATE` to the API runtime, nothing to the other logins, **no `DELETE`** (the cleanup of S0.19 under its own capability; `GRANT_CEILINGS` holds it there). A guard trigger keeps a record's identity, fingerprint and creation time, keeps a completed record's answer **byte for byte**, lets the sealed fields only be erased (together, with the tombstone set in the same update, never put back), and refuses a tombstone on a record in progress.
- *Same key, same request: the saved answer; same key, another request: `CONFLICT`; another principal or operation: its own namespace.* A replay returns the stored status and body byte for byte (all 256 byte values in a test). A command that began and did not finish is `IN_PROGRESS`, and its key meets `CONFLICT` for a different fingerprint in either state. A failed command rolls its record back with it, so the retry runs it again. **Two transactions beginning one key at once:** the second waits on the unique index, then gets the first's answer (`REPLAY`) once it commits, or owns the command (`NEW`) if the first rolled back; one winner, shown with two pooled connections and the second observed waiting on a lock in `pg_stat_activity`. `complete` completes only a command this transaction began (`SCOPE_NOT_OPEN`), once, and an answer that is not a status and bytes is refused.
- *The one secret replay (`registerRiderDevice`, and no other operation).* `complete` requires the secret fields for this operation and refuses them for every other (`SEALED_REPLAY_REFUSED`). They are sealed (AES-256-GCM, purpose `REPLAY_SECRET`, with the principal, the operation, the record and the envelope version as associated data via `sealedContext`) with the window in which they may be disclosed. `begin` returns them while the window is open **on the database's clock, read at the time** (`clock_timestamp()`), to the same principal, key and request, as often as it asks; after the window, or after `eraseSealedReplay`, it returns `REPLAY_UNAVAILABLE`, erasing an expired envelope on the read itself, so the command never runs twice while the record lives. An envelope moved to another record does not open (`AUTHENTICATION_FAILED`). No sentinel of the secret is in the row's text or in its bytes (searched as text and as hex); another principal with the same text key never receives it. `eraseSealedReplay` erases in the caller's transaction (it belongs to that transaction: one that rolls back leaves the secret), for the owner and for the `authentication` capability, and for nobody else.
- *Helpers.* `requestFingerprint` (SHA-256 of the canonical JSON of the operation, the path parameters and the body; key order does not matter, an absent body, `null`, `{}`, `[]` and `""` are five requests), and `etagOf` / `ifMatchVersion` / `versionMatches` (the ETag of a version is its number in quotes; `If-Match` is read as exactly one strong quoted decimal and nothing else).
- *Targeted breaks.* Fifteen single breaks (the fingerprint comparison, the in-progress answer, the disclosure window on read, the operation list of the exception, the principal check, the associated data of the envelope in both places, the "begun here" check, the erase result, the policy's principal binding, the `DELETE` grant, the guard's answer clause, the guard trigger, the `SYSTEM` arm of the update policy and the whole answer guard) fourteen were caught (the missing capability test of the `SYSTEM` arm by the migration lint and not by a database test) and each file was restored and its hash checked. The fifteenth (dropping only the `state` clause of the guard's answer check) is an **equivalent mutant**, which no test can tell from the original: the table's own CHECK on the answer already refuses a completed record set back to in progress, and the other clauses of the guard refuse the rest.
**Does not.** Apply the exception to any other operation, or cache a business response beyond its record.
**Stop if.** A second operation appears to need the exception (it is a contract change).

#### S0.13 — Settings reader and reason validator

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.9, S0.10 |
| **Run**| B D |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Newly builds.** A typed `Settings` reader over `setting` (a missing key is `SETTING_MISSING`, never a borrowed default, [settings.md](../../contracts/settings.md)); the **reason validator**: `REASON_REQUIRED` when empty, `REASON_NOT_ACTIVE` for an unknown, retired, foreign-domain or not-permitted-for-this-operation code, from the closed `permitted_operations` attribute, a branch-qualified entry (`operationId#BRANCH`) matching only the branch the use case passes, which it derives from recorded state under the identity lock and never from the client. Family O5.
**Files.** `platform/settings/*` and tests.
**Evidence.** DB tests per refusal; a reason of the wrong domain on each identity operation's list is refused, and a branch-qualified reason on the other branch is `REASON_NOT_ACTIVE`. *Fixture/platform verified*.
**Reached (10 October 2026), as `melarc_api_runtime` over a database seeded the way the product seeds it** (`settings-reason.db.test.ts`, 15 tests; `settings-reader.test.ts`, `reason-validator.test.ts`):
- *The settings reader.* `SettingsReader` reads in the caller's transaction, under the caller's context, with typed getters. **There is no default of any kind:** a key with no row, including every key the documents leave open (OB-78), is `SETTING_MISSING`; a stored value that is not of its declared type, or a request for a type other than the declared one, is `SETTING_INVALID` (it fails safely; the message never holds the value). All 31 seeded settings read back equal to the seed, in their types, scope and unit, and the six buckets and the idempotency TTL read as the contract states them (10, 10, 10, 20, 10, 120 a minute; 24 hours). A vendor, a rider or a `SYSTEM` context under another capability sees no row, so every key reads as missing there; the `authentication` capability and staff read.
- *The reason validator.* `decideReason` (pure) and `ReasonValidator` (reads the reason by its code). `REASON_REQUIRED` for no reason (absent, null, empty or blank); `REASON_NOT_ACTIVE` for a code that is not text or not of the grammar, unknown, retired, of another domain than the operation takes its reason from, or not permitted for the operation and branch by the closed `permitted_operations` attribute; `REASON_REQUIRED` again when the reason's metadata makes a note mandatory and none is given. A bare entry (`operationId`) is valid on every branch and with none; a qualified entry (`operationId#BRANCH`) only on the branch the use case passes, which it derives from recorded state under the identity lock and never from the client; an entry that merely begins like the operation permits nothing; a branch that is not one of the three is a defect of the caller. The refusal never repeats the code it was given. The operation-to-domain table (`IDENTITY_OPERATION_DOMAINS`) is read against domain-model.md §3.9 by a test.
- *Family O5, derived from the document.* All twelve identity reasons against all nine identity operations on every branch (and none): a reason is accepted exactly when its document list names the operation on that branch and the domain is the operation's, refused as `REASON_NOT_ACTIVE` otherwise; the three branch-qualified reasons are accepted on their own branch only. The two reason fixtures (`FIXTURE_REASON_INACTIVE`, `FIXTURE_REASON_OTHER_OPERATION`) are refused for a real operation.
- *Targeted breaks.* Nine single breaks (the domain check, the operation match, the active check, `null` read as a reason, the note rule, the missing-setting answer, the stored-type check, the asked-for-type check and the reason row read) were each caught, and each file was restored and its hash checked.
**Does not.** Build `upsertReasonDefinition` or any reason-management operation (a later slice); the identity domains are system-managed.
**Stop if.** A setting the slice reads has no approved value.

#### S0.14 — PostgreSQL-authoritative rate limiter

**Gate 2 correction (11 October 2026):** one transaction with the limit read in it (§11.B, migration `0012`), a fail-closed check on key versions (G2-04) and a capacity lock that makes the bound strict (G2-05); the cleanup election is gone. See [§7.15](#715-gate-2-audit-corrections-11-october-2026-disposition).

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.13, S0.5, S0.3, S0.6 |
| **Run**| B D |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Newly builds.** The `RateLimitAdmission` table (one row per admitted request, keyed by a digest, `DELETE` of expired rows only) and the `RateLimiter` over the **six canonical buckets** of [settings.md](../../contracts/settings.md) §7.7 and no seventh: a rolling 60-second window, per-key decisions serialised on database time, keyed digests under the limiter key with key-rotation overlap, bounded capacity that never evicts a live penalty, a request refused before it is counted when it should not count, **fail closed on a store outage**. It runs in its own short transaction and takes none of the policy locks. An in-memory adapter exists for unit tests only and production refuses it.
**Files.** `platform/limiter/*`, a new migration, the registry row, `.env.example` and tests.
**Evidence.** Two-process tests on one database (neither double-admits); an outage test asserting the refusal; a rotation test. *Fixture/platform verified*.
**Obligations carried.** Shared limiter at the deployed topology and trusted-proxy behaviour: release gate **G2**. The bucket launch values are those of [settings.md](../../contracts/settings.md) §7.7 and are not invented here; the limiter's own capacity bounds are development-qualified here.
**Reached (10 October 2026), as `melarc_api_runtime` opening the `rate_limit` capability** (`rate-limiter.db.test.ts`, 25 tests; `rate-limiter-processes.db.test.ts`, 2 tests over two Node processes; `admission-decision.test.ts`, `in-memory.rate-limiter.adapter.test.ts`, `limiter-digest.test.ts`, `lock-keys.test.ts`, the guard in `architecture.test.ts`):
- *The table.* `rate_limit_admission` (`0008_rate_limit_admission.sql`; the registry row `RateLimitAdmission`): one row per admitted request, keyed by a keyed digest of the bucket's key (HMAC-SHA-256 under the `limiter_digest` purpose, namespaced by bucket), the key version and the database's time; the bucket is one of the six names (CHECK), the digest 32 bytes. Row-level security admits a `SYSTEM` context under `rate_limit` alone, to read and insert, and to delete **only a row that has left the 60-second window**: the policy of `DELETE` carries the window, so a live row cannot be removed even by a mistake in the limiter (a test deletes as the capability: the live row survives, the expired one goes). Grants: `SELECT`, `INSERT` and `DELETE` to the API runtime, no `UPDATE`, nothing to the other logins; `GRANT_CEILINGS` holds it there. A staff, a vendor, `authentication` and every other context read nothing and write nothing.
- *The decision (`PostgresRateLimiter`), as first built (**changed by the Gate 2 correction, [§7.15](#715-gate-2-audit-corrections-11-october-2026-disposition): the limit is read in this transaction, a check that no live row is under a key version the instance does not hold is added, the cleanup election below no longer exists, and a capacity lock bounds every row, live or expired, in place of the live-rows-only capacity described here**).* One short transaction of its own, no caller transaction open, none of the policy locks: (1) a transaction-scoped advisory lock of class `RATE_LIMIT_KEY` on the digest under **every held key version**, taken in ascending order of key, so two instances that disagree on which version is active during a rotation still exclude each other through the one they share; (2) the removal of at most `CLEANUP_BATCH` expired rows **by the one admission that wins a try-lock** (class `RATE_LIMIT_CLEANUP`, 1296847410, pinned; an admission that loses it skips the cleaning and does not wait, because concurrent admissions choose the same expired rows and the second delete would wait for the first's commit, which made admissions for different keys queue; review finding 2). The cleaning is left, not dropped: the next admission wins and cleans, and capacity is judged on live rows, so a skipped cleaning costs nothing; (3) the live rows of the key under every held version, on the database's clock (one `clock_timestamp()` per statement); (4) the decision (`decideAgainstLimit`, pure): fewer live rows than the limit admits, at the limit the caller is told to wait until the row whose leaving frees a place has left, rounded up and never below one second; (5) if the table is at its capacity of live rows, **every admission is refused uniformly and nothing is evicted**; (6) one row under the active version. A refusal writes nothing, so it never extends a penalty. The consumption commits with this transaction, before the caller's work. The application's clock decides nothing (a limiter whose clock is ten days wrong admits the same ten).
- *The threshold, exact.* The six buckets admit exactly 10, 10, 10, 20, 10 and 120 in a window and refuse the next, read from the seeded settings. The window rolls (four of ten rows 70 seconds old free exactly four places). **Forty callers at once for one key admit exactly ten**; **two Node processes, each composing the real limiter from the built output, asking at the same moment (thirty requests each, five in flight), admit exactly ten between them with the rows ten and no process failing**, and a process started afterwards is given only what is left (nothing is held in memory). Each key and each bucket has its allowance; a key whose lock another connection holds does not hold up a different key, and **admissions for two different keys hold their key locks at the same moment** (both are stopped after their key locks by a table lock and `pg_locks` shows both granted, which one lock for all keys would not allow: review finding 8, a test that could not detect the regression it named).
- *Capacity.* A flood of distinct identifiers against a table at its cap is refused every time, evicts nothing, and the existing caller (itself under its limit) is refused too, uniformly; capacity returns as rows leave the window. The cleanup is a bounded batch per admission (250 expired rows: three admissions to clear them, one batch each). A key longer than 1024 bytes is refused uniformly without being digested or written.
- *Fail closed.* A closed pool, a write the store refuses, a lock that cannot be taken within the server's lock time, and a limit that is missing, not an integer, zero or beyond the cap each raise `LimiterUnavailableError` (`LIMITER_STORE_UNAVAILABLE`, the cause kept), admit nothing and write nothing; the next admission succeeds. There is no permissive fallback.
- *Rotation.* With version 1's rows counted, a limiter holding versions 1 and 2 with 2 active still refuses the key (the allowance is not reset); once those rows have left the window it admits and writes under version 2; a limiter holding only version 2 counts only version-2 rows.
- *What is stored.* A keyed digest, the bucket, the version and the time; the canonical key, the email in it and its local part are not in the row (searched as text). The same canonical key always digests to the same value and different keys differ.
- *The guard (OB-67 is closed).* The architecture guard for advisory locks now admits `platform/limiter/` beside the policy toolkit, takes the class from `lock-keys.ts` (`RATE_LIMIT_KEY`, 1296847409, and `RATE_LIMIT_CLEANUP`, 1296847410, pinned, distinct from the two policy classes and from each other) and refuses a lock whose class is a number written in the call. **The guard's rule on try-locks changed once, deliberately (OB-109):** `pg_try_advisory_*` stays refused everywhere, and the one exception is the transaction-scoped `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock` in `platform/limiter/` with a class from `lock-keys.ts` (one call in the limiter's own short transaction, never in a loop); the session-level and shared forms stay refused there too. **Superseded by the Gate 2 correction (G2-05):** the election was redundant once the capacity lock put every delete under one lock, so the exception is removed, `pg_try_advisory_*` is refused everywhere again, and `RATE_LIMIT_CLEANUP` (1296847410) is retired and never reused; the limiter's classes are `RATE_LIMIT_KEY` and `RATE_LIMIT_CAPACITY` (1296847411).
- *The in-memory limiter* (`InMemoryRateLimiter`): the same decision, bounds and refusals over memory, for unit tests and local diagnostics only; it is listed as an adapter, and `RATE_LIMITER=memory` is refused outside a local run by `adapter-refusals.ts`.
- *Counted.* A refusal is counted `rate_limited_total{bucket, cause}` (S0.15) after the commit, with the cause `BUCKET_CEILING`, `STORE_CAPACITY` or `KEY_TOO_LONG`; no identifier is in a label.
- *Targeted breaks.* Ten single breaks (the key lock removed, the lock class changed, the boundary of the decision, the policy of `DELETE` without the window, the capacity check, the lookup under every version, the fail-closed answer turned into an admission, the key bound, the bucket check, and the digest's namespace) were each caught, and each file was restored and its hash checked.
**Does not.** Key on a source address (there is no IP bucket).
**Stop if.** Serialising decisions per key costs more than the benchmark of S0.65 allows.

#### S0.15 — Metrics registry

**Gate 2 correction (11 October 2026):** a labels argument that is not a record is dropped in production and refused in development and test, and never throws from `Object.keys` (G2-08). See [§7.15](#715-gate-2-audit-corrections-11-october-2026-disposition).

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.1, S0.2 |
| **Run**| B |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Newly builds.** The `Metrics` adapter with **bounded labels** (an outcome class, never an identifier, address or token) and a test that fails when a label value is unbounded; the counters the specification names, for example `credential_recovery_requests_excluded_total{outcome}` ([OBSERVABILITY_AND_RECOVERY.md](../../architecture/OBSERVABILITY_AND_RECOVERY.md) §3). The private endpoint that serves them is S0.68.
**Files.** `platform/metrics/*` and tests.
**Evidence.** Unit tests on label cardinality and redaction. *Fixture/platform verified*.
**Obligations carried.** **OB-35, from the S0.1 review (9 October 2026):** the `Metrics` port raises `LABEL_UNBOUNDED` in development and test only, to fail a test on a programming defect. This task must define, implement and test what production does with a sample whose label value is outside its bounded set, record the choice in this block and report it in its handoff for the Product Owner. It must not leave production behaviour implicit.
**Reached (10 October 2026)** (`bounded.metrics.adapter.test.ts`, 20 tests, and `metric-catalogue.test.ts`, 6 tests; the counting of the audit writer and of both limiters in their database and unit tests):
- *The registry.* `BoundedMetrics` (`platform/metrics/`) over a **closed catalogue** (`metric-catalogue.ts`, frozen to the value sets): a metric is one the catalogue defines, a sample names every label of its metric and no other, and every value is one of the label's closed set, so the series a metric can have are the product of small fixed sets and no identifier, address, destination or token can be a label. The catalogue holds `credential_recovery_requests_excluded_total{outcome}` (the outcomes `UNKNOWN_OR_INELIGIBLE`, `RATE_LIMITED`, `REJECTED`, compared with OBSERVABILITY_AND_RECOVERY.md §3 by a test), `rate_limited_total{bucket, cause}` (the six buckets; the causes `BUCKET_CEILING`, `STORE_CAPACITY`, `KEY_TOO_LONG`, `HASH_ADMISSION_FULL`), `audit_enhanced_write_failed_total` and the registry's own `metrics_samples_dropped_total{reason}`. Counters end in `_total`; no label is named for an identifier. Nothing is served: the private endpoint, the thresholds and the alerts are S0.68's, and no endpoint exists here.
- *OB-35, resolved and recorded: what production does with a sample outside the catalogue.* In development and test the registry **throws** (`LABEL_UNBOUNDED`; a wrong kind of call or a value that is not a number is `METRIC_SAMPLE_INVALID`), so a programming defect fails a test. In production (`labelPolicyFor('production')`) it **drops the sample and goes on**: counting never fails the operation it counts (a request that has just committed a credential change does not become a failure because a label was wrong). The drop is not silent: it is counted in `metrics_samples_dropped_total` by reason, and a **bounded, redacted diagnostic** goes to the sink once per metric and reason, then at most once an interval of 60 seconds on the monotonic clock with the number held back. The error and the diagnostic carry the metric's name and the label's name, taken from the catalogue, and **never a value**; a name the catalogue does not hold is reported as `(unknown)`. A diagnostic sink that itself fails (a full log, a closed stream) does not make the counting fail: the drop is already counted, and the failure of the sink is not raised (review finding 7). Nothing is stored per bad sample: a thousand distinct bad names and values leave the registry's state exactly as one would. If an authoritative rule conflicts with dropping (none was found in the documents read), it is reported before different semantics are adopted.
- *Wired where the specification names a counter.* The audit writer counts an enhanced event that could not be written (the act did not happen) and counts no other; both limiters count their refusals after the commit.
- *Targeted breaks.* Seven single breaks (the policy, the label value check, the diagnostic interval, the drop counter, the label-set check, the production policy, and the enhanced-only count) were each caught, and each file was restored and its hash checked.
**Does not.** Expose metrics publicly.
**Stop if.** A required metric needs an identifier as a label.

#### S0.16 — Bounded hash admission

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.14, S0.3, S0.15 |
| **Run**| B D |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Newly builds.** `HashAdmission`: admission before any identity-dependent work, a bounded number of concurrent password hashes, and when full an immediate `429 RATE_LIMITED` **without verifying and without incrementing a counter**; a test-only workload fixture. The slot count is a development-qualified engineering value chosen and recorded here; deployed capacity is S0.65's proposal.
**Files.** `platform/limiter/hash-admission*` and tests.
**Evidence.** Saturation test: the refused request does no hash and writes no counter; admitted work completes. *Fixture/platform verified*.
**Reached (10 October 2026)** (`bounded.hash-admission.adapter.test.ts`, 13 tests; `hash-admission-config.test.ts`, 5; `hash-admission.db.test.ts`, 4, over a real database and the real Argon2id hasher):
- *The bound.* `BoundedHashAdmission.tryAdmit()` answers at once with a slot or with `null`: **there is no waiting queue** (until a measured, bounded waiting policy is recorded). A slot is released once however often it is released. `runAdmitted(admission, work)` does not call the work at all when no slot is free (nothing is hashed, no dummy hash, nothing counted; the caller answers `RATE_LIMITED`) and gives the slot back when the work finishes or fails.
- *Saturation, against a real database and the real hasher.* With both slots held, eight sign-in steps all answer `RATE_LIMITED` while the slots are still held, the hasher is called **zero** times and no failed-attempt row is written; with six arriving and two slots, exactly two are admitted (two derivations, two counters written) and four are refused with neither; the slots are free afterwards and a correct secret is verified; never more than two derivations are in flight. The refusal is counted `rate_limited_total{bucket, cause: HASH_ADMISSION_FULL}` by the code that answers it (the bucket of the operation is known there, not here).
- *The memory of the largest accepted profile, not the write profile.* `HASH_ADMISSION_SLOTS` (default 2, 1 to 64) and `HASH_ADMISSION_MEMORY_BUDGET_MIB` (default 256, 64 to 65536) are configuration, validated at start with a relation: the slots times the memory of the **largest profile that may be derived** (the write profile and every `ARGON2_ACCEPTED_PROFILES` entry) must fit the budget. Three slots of a 64 MiB write profile fit 256 MiB; three slots refused when one accepted older profile is 128 MiB (384 MiB); a small write profile with a large accepted one is judged by the large one; the relation holds in staging and production as in a local run; a refusal names the key and no figure of a profile.
- *Shared libuv capacity.* Argon2id runs on the libuv pool the process shares. The admission refuses to be composed if the slots plus the threads it keeps back (`DEFAULT_RESERVED_THREADS`, 1) exceed the process's pool (`libuvThreadpoolSize`, read as Node reads `UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE`); The pool size is read **as libuv reads it, with C's `atoi`**: absent is 4; present, the leading integer, a size of 0 becomes 1 and a negative number a very large pool capped at 1024, so `0`, `abc` and an empty value are a pool of one thread (the first version of this reading took them for 4; the review of this batch doubted it and a timing experiment on Node 24.21.0 showed it wrong, DEPLOYMENT_AND_ENVIRONMENTS.md §12.5). `planHashAdmission` works out the worst-case memory, the threads left and refuses a plan that exceeds either. **Measured on the development machine and recorded with its environment (DEPLOYMENT_AND_ENVIRONMENTS.md §12.5):** memory grows by 64 MiB for each concurrent hash at the development profile; with the whole pool of four taken by hashes, other libuv work waited 174 to 448 ms, with three of four it waited about 1 ms; two hashes at once take a median 160 ms each. These are the reasons for a reserve of one thread and a default of two slots, and they are **development-qualified, not production-qualified**; S0.65 calibrates them on the deployment machine.
- *Targeted breaks.* Seven single breaks (the slot boundary, the double release, the pool relation, the largest-accepted-profile memory in the plan and in the configuration, the work run when refused, and the slot not given back) were each caught, and each file was restored and its hash checked.
**Does not.** Add dummy hashing to a rate-limited or admission-refused request (a locked request still does one, S0.32).
**Stop if.** Admission limits starve legitimate sign-in at the benchmark's expected load.

#### S0.17 — Durable credential delivery: schema, enqueue, state machine

**Gate 2 correction (11 October 2026):** the column `deferred_claims`, the outcome class `PAYLOAD_DEFERRED` and the guard rule that lets a claim be given back only by recording a deferral (G2-03, migration `0011`); `state-machines.md` is unchanged, since no state and no transition pair was added. See [§7.15](#715-gate-2-audit-corrections-11-october-2026-disposition).

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.11, S0.5, S0.3 |
| **Run**| B D |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Newly builds.** `credential_delivery_job` ([state-machines.md](../../contracts/state-machines.md) §22: `QUEUED`, `LEASED`, `ACCEPTED`, `RETRYABLE`, `FAILED`, `EXPIRED`, `CANCELLED`); lease owner and version fencing; a stable delivery identity (the dedupe id) that every attempt of the job reuses, so a retry is the same message to the provider; an **encrypted minimal payload** bound to its row by authenticated data; enqueue inside the caller's transaction beside the hash-only redemption record; cancellation and invalidation; the capability and registry rows ([SECURITY_DESIGN.md](../../architecture/SECURITY_DESIGN.md) §13.9a).
**Files.** `platform/delivery/*`, a new migration, the registry row, and tests.
**Evidence.** DB tests against a stand-in redemption record (a test table, because the real `setup_grant` and `recovery_request` arrive in S0.29 and S0.50; the `PENDING` check of the state machine runs against it): an enqueue rolls back with its transaction; a job's state never changes the redemption record; a fenced-out claimant cannot finish a job. *Fixture/platform verified*.
**Reached (10 October 2026)** (`credential-delivery-job.db.test.ts`, 140 tests, over a real database as the technical logins and with a stand-in redemption record in a test table; `delivery-payload.test.ts`, 60; `delivery-schedule.test.ts`, 14; `worker-job-store.test.ts`, 2; and the capability, provisioning and catalogue suites that pin the new grants):
- *The table.* `credential_delivery_job` (migration `0009`; class SYSTEM in the registry): one queued message per redemption record (unique on the record's type and id), a stable provider deduplication identity (unique; every attempt of the job reuses it), the seven states of state-machines.md §22, the lease (owner, version, end), the count of attempts, the next attempt and the expiry (the redemption record's, immutable), and the destination and the link only as a **sealed payload** (ciphertext, nonce, key version, envelope version) that is **erased when the job ends**. CHECK constraints hold what the guard also holds: a live job holds its payload and a finished one holds none and says when it was erased; each state accompanies only the outcome classes it may; a recovery link serves a `RecoveryRequest` and every other kind a `SetupGrant`; a queued job is untouched. The table is owned by `melarc_owner`, row-level security is enabled and forced, and no login holds `DELETE` or `TRUNCATE` (the registry gives none: OB-113, OB-114).
- *Who reaches it.* No staff, vendor or rider context reads or writes a row (the closed `ELSE` of every policy). Two SYSTEM capabilities do, in policies of their own: `authentication` (the API, in the transaction that issues or ends a credential) inserts a `QUEUED` job, reads five key columns (not even the job's identifier) and cancels a job that can still change; `credential_delivery` (the worker) reads the whole row and updates a job that can still change, and holds no `INSERT`. The API cannot read the payload, the destination or the lease of a job it queued (OB-115). The worker's login holds its capability through `melarc_worker_cap_credential_delivery` (OB-50).
- *The guard.* A `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger keeps the state machine whoever writes: a job never changes who it is for, what it serves or when it ends; a job that has ended never changes; a claim takes a lease of at most an hour only when the job is due (or its earlier lease has ended) and the redemption record has not expired, counts one attempt, raises the lease version by one and leaves the payload alone; an outcome leaves the lease and the count as they were, which is what the writer's fence compared; a payload is erased and **never replaced**, whatever the transition; and the worker's capability ends (cancels) only a job it holds the lease of, where the consuming operation cancels a queued one (OB-126). The guard function belongs to `melarc_owner` and no one else may execute it.
- *Enqueue and cancel.* `PostgresDeliveryQueue.enqueue` writes in the caller's transaction through its handle, so a message and its record commit together or not at all (an enqueue rolls back with its transaction). Both it and `cancel` refuse, before anything is written, a transaction that is not the issuing capability's (`DELIVERY_CONTEXT_INVALID`): under another context a cancellation would find no row to change and report none cancelled, which reads as "nothing was live". It seals the destination and the link under the `DELIVERY_PAYLOAD` key with authenticated data that binds the job, the kind, the principal and the redemption record (a ciphertext moved to another row does not open), and refuses what could not be delivered (`DELIVERY_NOT_QUEUEABLE`: a destination that is not an address or a number in the form of E.164 for its channel, a link that is not an absolute http or https URL without credentials, a kind that does not serve the record's type, a second message for one record, a credential that has already ended) without echoing a value. `cancel` ends the live jobs of a record or of a principal and erases their payloads, in the transaction that consumed, superseded or invalidated the record.
- *Fencing and the retry decision.* A claim returns the lease version, and an outcome is written only against it, so a claimant whose lease was taken over, or whose job was cancelled, finishes nothing (`LEASE_LOST`). Whether an unknown outcome waits or ends is decided in the statement that records it, on the database's clock, **read once for the decision**: the Class 1 schedule (30 s, 2 min, 10 min, 30 min, 2 h; six attempts; ±20 % jitter), a provider's `Retry-After` lengthening a wait and never removing the bound, and the end of the redemption record as the cutoff. A job claimed after the sixth attempt ends as `SCHEDULE_EXHAUSTED` (as `EXPIRED` if its credential has ended) without a send.
- *Payloads this worker cannot open.* A claim whose payload would not open reports the lease and why: a payload that no worker can open (it does not authenticate as this row's, or is not what is written) ends the job as `PAYLOAD_UNREADABLE`; one that only this worker cannot open (a key, envelope or layout version it has not been given, as in a rotation under way) is kept and tried again on the schedule, because an erased payload cannot be recovered.
- *The checks of the task.* A fenced-out claimant cannot finish a job (the takeover test). That a job's state never changes the redemption record it serves is shown by the shape of the table, because the real record tables arrive in S0.29 and S0.50: it is tied to no other table by a key, and the only thing that runs when a job changes is a guard that reads and writes nothing else. The `PENDING` guards of the state machine (the issuing operation's, S0.29; the claim's, the worker's reader, OB-119) have no production implementation yet. The privileges of each login are pinned column by column.
- *Targeted breaks.* 89 single breaks of the guard, the policies, the constraints, the fence, the retry decision, the enqueue and the cancellation: 61 were caught at first, one matched nothing (and was corrected) and 27 were not caught; each of those was closed with a test that asserts the message of the guard it covers (a policy test uses a constant `UPDATE`, because a statement that reads a column brings the `SELECT` policies in), except four: the retry's `NULL` schedule end (the same under `NULL` semantics), the cancellation's "only live states" (the policy already hides ended rows), the guard function's public `EXECUTE` (caught by the catalogue test and not by the job suite) and "due now" on a relinquished job (the code was removed). The changes of the review ([§7.14](#714-carried-from-s017-to-s019-batch-of-10-october-2026)) were each broken on purpose and caught.
**Does not.** Call a provider, or write a `RecoveryRequest` or `SetupGrant` (S0.29, S0.50).
**Stop if.** The redemption record would need to hold the destination.

#### S0.18 — Delivery worker process

**Gate 2 correction (11 October 2026):** the capture sink is judged by path component (G2-01); a late provider answer is never timely (G2-02); a payload this worker cannot open is a deferral, and a worker with no redemption reader claims nothing (G2-03); a local run refuses a real provider (§11.A). See [§7.15](#715-gate-2-audit-corrections-11-october-2026-disposition).

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.17, S0.15, S0.6 |
| **Run**| B D L |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Newly builds.** A separate worker process: claim with a lease in a short transaction, release the connection, re-read cancellation and expiry, call `DeliveryProvider` with no transaction open, and record the outcome in another short transaction as *accepted*, *rejected* or **unknown**. **An unknown outcome is a normal case, not a stop**: a timeout or a lost acknowledgement may mean the provider accepted the message ([SECURITY_DESIGN.md](../../architecture/SECURITY_DESIGN.md) §13.9a), so the job is retried with the **same link and the same stable delivery identity**, or reconciled where the provider offers a status query, on the bounded schedule cut off at the redemption record's expiry. Lease ownership and version **fence** a stale worker from overwriting a later outcome; a send already in flight after a cancellation is dead because redemption rejects it; diagnostics are redacted (no token, destination or message body). Exactly-once delivery is **not** promised, and a fresh credential is **never** generated merely because an acknowledgement was lost. Shutdown stops claiming and relinquishes leases within the budget; the **capture adapter** (a sink outside the repository, restricted, never inside `package:source`) is the default in Local and Staging; ciphertext is erased after acceptance, or at terminal expiry or cancellation; the `DESTINATION_NOT_ALLOWED` outcome class serves the controlled real-provider window ([DEPLOYMENT_AND_ENVIRONMENTS.md](../../architecture/DEPLOYMENT_AND_ENVIRONMENTS.md) §4.1).
**Files.** `platform/delivery/worker/*`, a worker entry point beside `main.ts`, `apps/api/package.json`, `.env.example`, [DEVELOPMENT.md](../../DEVELOPMENT.md), and tests.
**Evidence.** Crash-after-commit, lease-expiry, duplicate-claim and timeout-then-late-acceptance tests, and **the accepted-but-unacknowledged send** (the provider keeps the message, the acknowledgement is lost): the retry carries the same link and delivery identity, nothing new is issued, and a second delivery is tolerated because redemption is single-use; stale-worker fencing; cancellation before and after the send; redacted diagnostics (a planted token never appears in a log line or an error); no connection held during a send; a terminal failure is counted by a metric (the alerts and their rehearsal are S0.68). A real `SIGTERM` stop **needs Linux, WSL2 or CI** (skipped on Windows).
**Obligations carried.** `L`: the shutdown test on Linux. The real-provider window and credentials are release gate **G3**; no provider is contacted here.
**Reached (10 October 2026)** (`delivery-worker.test.ts`, 76 tests; `worker-config.test.ts`, 67; `delivery-worker-runtime.test.ts`, 19; `capture-sink.test.ts`, 19 and 1 skipped, which also holds the capture and allow-list adapters' cases; `delivery-allow-list.test.ts`, 11; `outside-checkout.test.ts`, 20 and 1 skipped; `worker-local-env.test.ts`, 9; `delivery-worker.db.test.ts`, 21, over a real database as `melarc_worker_runtime` and `melarc_api_runtime`; `delivery-worker.process.test.ts`, 6 and 1 skipped, and `delivery-worker-process.db.test.ts`, 3 and 1 skipped, which start the built worker; the redemption record is the stand-in table of S0.17 and the provider behaves as the evidence list needs it to):
- *The process.* `delivery-worker-main.ts` starts the worker (`pnpm --filter @melarc/api run start:worker`; `start:worker:local` reads the same `apps/api/.env` as the API). It has no listener and no Nest container, and its own composition root (`delivery-worker-runtime.ts`, in `COMPOSITION_ROOT`). It connects as `melarc_worker_runtime` over a pool of its own (the lanes plus two) under an application name of its own, and holds the `credential_delivery` key and no other (`AesGcmSecretCipher` takes the purposes it serves). Its settings are validated at start, and again when a configuration is made by hand, and a problem names the key and never a value (DEPLOYMENT_AND_ENVIRONMENTS.md §12.5, the worker's table and refusals; OB-117).
- *One job.* A lane claims a due job with a lease in a short transaction (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, on the database's clock) and lets the connection go; asks the provider's status first, after the first attempt or after taking a lease over; **re-reads the redemption record just before the send** (a closed record ends the job and one it cannot read waits on the schedule, and **nothing is sent for either**); calls the provider with **no transaction open**; and records *accepted*, *rejected* or *unknown* in another short transaction against the lease version it was given. A stale worker, or one whose job was cancelled meanwhile, writes nothing (`LEASE_LOST`) and says so. A job that has ended holds no ciphertext.
- *Unknown is a normal case.* A timeout, a thrown error, a lost acknowledgement or an answer that is none of the three is `UNKNOWN`; the retry carries **the same link and the same deduplication identity**, nothing new is issued, and a second delivery after an accepted-but-unacknowledged send is tolerated because redemption is single-use. Shown against a real database: a crash after commit, lease expiry and takeover, two workers asking at once (one job, one winner), a late acceptance after a timeout, the provider that keeps the message and loses the acknowledgement (the status query finds it and nothing is sent twice), cancellation before the claim and after the send began, a payload that will not open, a key version the worker does not hold (the message is kept), and a record that closed. **The schedule ends at the sixth attempt:** a lease taken over after a crash and a lease given back at a stop each count as an attempt, so a message claimed a seventh time is asked about once more and then ends, and nothing is sent.
- *Time.* The work on a job runs under an `ExecutionBudget` of its own that ends before the lease does (lease = 30 s plus the longest provider timeout; the budget is the lease less 5 s, **counted from the start of the claim** because the lease begins inside it). The read of the record and each provider call are effects of that budget with a budget for the call (OB-56, OB-57), and **a call is late when its budget says so on the monotonic clock** (shown with a clock that runs at half speed), at which moment its signal is aborted: a provider that ignores its signal cannot hold a lane or a lease, and one that answers late is logged and changes nothing. The outcome is written under a fresh short budget, and a status query that used up the job's time leaves the job to the schedule, not to an immediate retry. No transaction is open and no statement runs while a provider is being called. The worker's own login repeats the cancel test (OB-58).
- *Providers.* The capture adapter (the default in Local and Staging) writes each message to a folder outside the checkout, in clear by design, once per deduplication identity (OB-124); the worker refuses a folder inside the checkout **however the path leads there** (a link or a junction is followed), and an existing folder that other users can enter or that another user owns (on a file system with permission bits); production refuses the adapter. In a controlled window the allow-list guard refuses a destination not on the list with `DESTINATION_NOT_ALLOWED` before any provider call. **No real provider is built** (G3), and the worker refuses to start with one selected instead of standing a stub in its place (OB-120). **The reader of the redemption records has no implementation** until S0.29 and S0.50, so the worker confirms nothing, sends nothing and says so when it starts (OB-119).
- *Diagnostics.* A log line holds a job's id, channel, kind and attempt, an outcome class, and a failure's class and code: never a destination, a link, a token or the text of an error. A planted token appears in no log line, error or process output (shown in the unit, database and process tests).
- *Metrics.* `credential_delivery_attempts_total{channel, result}`, `credential_delivery_jobs_failed_total{outcome}` (the four terminal failures) and `credential_delivery_leases_expired_total` (a takeover, and a lease that ended on a job the cleanup expired), bounded by the catalogue. The gauge and the alerts are S0.68's (OB-122).
- *Shutdown.* On `SIGTERM` or `SIGINT` the worker stops claiming, lets the jobs in flight finish for most of `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS`, gives back the leases of those still running (they are due again at once, and the attempt counts: OB-118) and exits by itself within the budget. **After the stop nothing is sent and nothing is written in its name:** the budget every job hangs from is finished, which refuses what a lane that was given up on would start, and the calls the lanes were waiting on are let go. The report says what was given back and what could not be confirmed, each for what it is. **On Windows the stop was shown with a worker that signals itself; the stop through the operating system's `SIGTERM` is written and skipped there, and is required on Linux, WSL2 or CI before Gate 2 (OB-24).**
- *Targeted breaks.* The campaign of S0.18 and S0.19 was 82 single breaks of the configuration, the least-privilege cipher, the sink, the allow-list, the worker, the runtime, the logger and the metrics (and, for S0.19, the cleanup and its migration), each file restored and its hash checked: 78 were caught at first, and each of the other four was closed with a test and caught on a second run. The changes of the review ([§7.14](#714-carried-from-s017-to-s019-batch-of-10-october-2026)) were broken the same way (see there).
- *The kit (OB-88).* The capture adapter writes to the integration kit's reader through `deliverySinkOver`; five breaks of that binding were caught.
**Does not.** Contact a real provider, or hold a transaction across a send.
**Stop if.** The worker cannot give the guarantees above — fencing, same-link retry under a stable delivery identity, cancellation checks, redacted diagnostics — with the pinned PostgreSQL and the provider port. Ordinary provider ambiguity is not a stop: it is the case this design is for.

#### S0.19 — Retention cleanup (Gate 2)

**Gate 2 correction (11 October 2026):** the replay cleanup is served by a partial index and a stable bound (G2-07, migration `0013`); erasing material, replay guarantees and metadata retention are three things and S0.19 is complete for the first and part of the second only (§11.E). See [§7.15](#715-gate-2-audit-corrections-11-october-2026-disposition).

| | |
|---|---|
| **Needs**| S0.18, S0.12, S0.16 |
| **Run**| B D |
| **Operations**| none |
| **Criteria**| none |

**Newly builds.** A durable cleanup task for replay ciphertext, expired and terminal jobs and tombstones, under its own capability.
**Files.** `platform/delivery/retention/*` and tests.
**Evidence.** DB tests: only expired material is removed; a live row survives; the task is idempotent. **Gate 2 exit:** S0.11 to S0.19 green under `pnpm run test:db`, and the worker's real `SIGTERM` shutdown test run on Linux, WSL2 or CI. That Linux evidence is **new** and, by the Product Owner's decision of 9 October 2026 (§10 row 13), **required at this gate with no waiver**: until it is run Gate 2 is **pending**, not passed. **Retention cleanup is mandatory and comes first:** S0.29, the first task that builds grant-issuing code, needs this task, so the bootstrap seed (S0.30), the MFA continuation grants (S0.31), S0.36 and every later issuing task cannot be accepted without it.
**Reached (10 October 2026)** (`retention-cleanup.db.test.ts`, 18 tests, over a real database as `melarc_worker_runtime`; `retention-cleanup.test.ts`, 13; `retention-task.test.ts`, 8; `delivery-worker-process.db.test.ts`, which starts the built worker):
- *What it erases, and what it does not delete.* Under the `credential_delivery` capability (the registry's task `erase_expired_credential_material`; no new capability: OB-125), run by the worker on a schedule beside its lanes (migration `0010`; `platform/delivery/retention/`): (1) a delivery job whose redemption record has ended and that no worker is working on (queued, waiting to retry, or leased with a lease that has ended) becomes `EXPIRED`, and its payload, which holds a live token, is erased (a lease that had ended with no outcome is counted, because no takeover will come for the job); (2) the sealed replay of an idempotency record whose window has closed (`sealed_expires_at`, on the database's clock) is erased, all of it together, with the tombstone (`replay_unavailable_at`) set and the saved answer and the request's hash kept byte for byte. **It deletes no row of any table**, because the registry gives no identity `DELETE` on either (OB-113, OB-114), and it touches no audit row.
- *Only what has expired.* A live job and a record whose window is open survive, and so does a job whose lease is still held although its record has ended (it is the sending worker's until the lease ends); a record with no sealed replay is left alone; a second run finds nothing to do (idempotent). A run takes at most `RETENTION_BATCH_SIZE` rows of each kind in one short transaction each, skips rows another cleanup holds locked instead of waiting for them, and goes on while a batch was full, up to 20 rounds, **under a budget of its own (30 s) inside the worker's**; a stop ends the run between rounds and cancels what is in flight.
- *The privileges.* The migration gives the worker's capability role `SELECT` on two columns of `idempotency_record` (the identity and the end of the window) and `UPDATE` on the six an erasure changes, with a read policy for a record that holds a sealed replay or says it held one and an update policy that admits only an erasure **of a replay whose window has closed**, so that a statement of the capability's own cannot erase a live one. The worker cannot read the sealed bytes, the saved answer or whose record it is, cannot complete a command in progress, change an answer or lengthen a window, and holds neither `INSERT` nor `DELETE`; the guard trigger of `0007` holds every updater to the same rules. The capability matrix lists `platform/delivery/retention/` as a place the capability is opened.
- *The stop condition and the capability.* "Retention periods are unset in settings.md": the part of the task that would need an unset period (deleting finished jobs, and tombstoned records) is not built and is reported as the decision needed (OB-114, OB-113); the independent part, erasing material whose own end has passed, uses only values that are set and invents no duration.
